========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this sense that I’ve messed things up, especially for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep thinking that was my fault, like I pushed us into something I should’ve known better about, and now every bill or little problem with the house feels like proof that I’ve let them down.

And it’s not just at home. At work, if I make even a small mistake—like getting an order wrong or forgetting something for a table—I just immediately go to this place in my head where it means I’m useless. Like it’s not just “I made a mistake,” it’s “of course you did, because you’re no good.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels true. I keep feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself somehow.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it would just disappear, no. I mean… it would help, obviously. If the mortgage were somehow fixed tomorrow, I’d probably feel some relief, like one big thing wasn’t sitting on my chest all the time.

But the way I feel about it has kind of spread, if that makes sense. It’s not only “we have this mortgage and that’s stressful.” It’s more like I took that as proof that I let my family down, and then I started seeing everything else through that same lens. Like if something small happens at work, if I mess up an order or forget something for a table, I don’t just think, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I go straight to, “See, you’re useless. This is what you do. You ruin things.” And then I feel like whatever bad thing happens after that, I probably deserve it somehow.

So… resolving the mortgage would take away one big trigger, I guess. But I’m not sure it would fix the part of me that keeps turning everything into evidence that I’ve failed people.

been all over the place, where some weeks I barely touch staff meal and other nights I come home and eat whatever’s in the kitchen just to feel settled.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: People at work have noticed I either pace around checking the same tables and receipts over and over, or I get so slowed down that grabbing menus and walking to the POS takes me longer than it should.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I’m having trouble keeping numbers straight on the POS and inventory sheets, and I’ll stare at the screen knowing I’ve done this for years but not trusting what I just entered.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to hurt myself, but when I’m lying awake before an early shift I sometimes think it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up and didn’t have to keep failing at everything.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During the worst depressed stretches, the voice and the belief that the bank or my manager was watching me were still there, so I was dealing with both at the same time while trying to get through my shifts.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of this whole period, even It became this constant thing in the back of my mind, like every problem somehow pointed back to me making that choice.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were resolved tomorrow, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would just disappear, no. I mean… it would help, obviously. If the mortgage were somehow fixed tomorrow, I’d probably feel some relief, like one big thing wasn’t sitting on my chest all the time.\n\nBut the way I feel about it has kind of spread, if that makes sense. Like if something small happens at work, if I mess up an order or forget something for a table, I don’t just think, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I go straight to, “See, you’re useless. This is what you do. You ruin things.” And then I feel like whatever bad thing happens after that, I probably deserve it somehow.\n\nSo… resolving the mortgage would take away one big trigger, I guess. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. It’s kind of embarrassing to admit because it sounds small, but I notice it in those little everyday things.

Like at work, I used to enjoy joking around with the regulars. There are people who come in all the time, and I used to remember their little stories or tease them about ordering the same thing, just harmless stuff. It made the shift feel a little lighter. Now I mostly just keep my face straight, take the order, bring the food, say “sure” or “no problem,” and that’s it. I’m not rude, I don’t think, but I don’t really feel that pull to connect or make it fun anymore.

And after shifts, I used to text my brother, even if it was just something stupid that happened at work or a picture of something funny. Now I’ll see his message sitting there and I just… leave it. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel the energy or the interest to answer, even when part of me knows I should. Then too much time passes and it feels harder to respond, so I let it sit longer.

So yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest in things I used to actually get something out of. I still do what I have to do, but I don’t really reach for things the way I used to.

Yeah… I mean, I don’t have a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.

But… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.

It scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.

Most of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.

I guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.

description: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: I used to joke with regulars and text my brother after shifts, but now I mostly keep my face straight, say only what I have to, and let messages sit because I don’t feel the pull to answer.\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most days there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like I’m going through the motions carrying plates and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I feel anything good.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: My eating has been all over the place, where some weeks I barely touch staff meal and other nights I come home and eat whatever’s in the kitchen just to feel settled.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: People at work have noticed I either pace around checking the same tables and receipts over and over, or I get so slowed down that grabbing menus and walking to the POS takes me longer than it should.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I’m having trouble keeping numbers straight on the POS and inventory sheets, and I’ll stare at the screen knowing I’ve done this for years but not trusting what I just entered.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to hurt myself, but when I’m lying awake before an early shift I sometimes think it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up and didn’t have to keep failing at everything.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During the worst depressed stretches, the voice and the belief that the bank or my manager was watching me were still there, so I was dealing with both at the same time while trying to get through my shifts.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of this whole period, even when some days were lighter, I’ve been weighed down by that empty, guilty feeling more often than not.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood was not especially depressed and I was getting through work better, but I still heard the whispering at the condo and still felt watched over the mortgage paperwork.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve had near-missed shifts, mistakes with mortgage paperwork, and more trouble keeping up with POS reports and inventory, which makes me worry I could lose my job and then the condo. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.\n\nBut… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. Or at least it’s the one I’m really noticing now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.

I mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.

It kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.

So… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to “most of my adult life.” It feels more like something that comes up at certain times, not something I can clearly say has always been there.

I still do what I have to do, but I don’t really reach for things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t have a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.\n\nBut… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. Or at least it’s the one I’m really noticing now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m trying not to overcomplicate it, but I still don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.

Before this current thing, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of dragged out, where I wasn’t myself and I didn’t want to deal with much. But when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” and “like this,” that’s where I get stuck. I can’t really point to a specific stretch and say, yes, that was definitely the same kind of depression and it lasted that long.

I’m not saying it never happened, because my memory of that stuff isn’t perfect. I don’t really mark it on a calendar or anything. But I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods. Some of them might have been more like stress, or being tired, or just having a bad run of days.

So I guess the most honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear past episode I can identify like that before this one.

It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to “most of my adult life.” It feels more like something that comes up at certain times, not something I can clearly say has always been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m trying not to overcomplicate it, but I still don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nBefore this current thing, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of dragged out, where I wasn’t myself and I didn’t want to deal with much. But when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” and “like this,” that’s where I get stuck. I can’t really point to a specific stretch and say, yes, that was definitely the same kind of depression and it lasted that long.\n\nI’m not saying it never happened, because my memory of that stuff isn’t perfect. I don’t really mark it on a calendar or anything. But I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods. Some of them might have been more like stress, or being tired, or just having a bad run of days.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is… I’m not sure. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. Not in a clear way, at least.

I’ve talked to people before—like, not always regularly, and sometimes it was more just because I was having a hard time or needed to sort things out—but I don’t remember anyone sitting me down and saying, “You are depressed,” or giving it to me like an official thing. Maybe they used words like stress or feeling low, or asked me about my mood, but I can’t point to a time before this where a doctor or therapist specifically told me I was depressed.

I guess it’s possible I’m forgetting some wording from years ago, because I don’t always remember the exact language people use in appointments. But if you mean, was I diagnosed or clearly told that before this started, then no, I don’t really have that memory.

It became this constant thing in the back of my mind, like every problem somehow pointed back to me making that choice.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were resolved tomorrow, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would just disappear, no. Like if something small happens at work, if I mess up an order or forget something for a table, I don’t just think, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I go straight to, “See, you’re useless. This is what you do. But I’m not sure it would fix the part of me that keeps turning everything into evidence that I’ve failed people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest a lot of the time. Like I’m moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAt work I can still carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say the little polite things you’re supposed to say, but it feels kind of… mechanical. Like I’m doing the part of a person who’s fine, but inside I don’t really feel much that’s good. It’s more like I’m just getting through each hour.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s kind of embarrassing to admit because it sounds small, but I notice it in those little everyday things.\n\nLike at work, I used to enjoy joking around with the regulars. There are people who come in all the time, and I used to remember their little stories or tease them about ordering the same thing, just harmless stuff. It made the shift feel a little lighter. Now I mostly just keep my face straight, take the order, bring the food, say “sure” or “no problem,” and that’s it. I’m not rude, I don’t think, but I don’t really feel that pull to connect or make it fun anymore.\n\nAnd after shifts, I used to text my brother, even if it was just something stupid that happened at work or a picture of something funny. Now I’ll see his message sitting there and I just… leave it. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel the energy or the interest to answer, even when part of me knows I should. Then too much time passes and it feels harder to respond, so I let it sit longer.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest in things I used to actually get something out of. I still do what I have to do, but I don’t really reach for things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t have a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.\n\nBut… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. could lose my job and then the condo. I’ve also pulled back from my brother and coworkers enough that people have started noticing I’m not myself.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 8 months, since after I took on the condo mortgage.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The voices and fixed fears about being watched have been happening for about 1 year and 4 months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The depressed, guilty periods have been present for about 1 year and 2 months of this whole time.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to “most of my adult life.” It feels more like something that comes up at certain times, not something I can clearly say has always been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m trying not to overcomplicate it, but I still don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nBefore this current thing, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of dragged out, where I wasn’t myself and I didn’t want to deal with much. But when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” and “like this,” that’s where I get stuck. I can’t really point to a specific stretch and say, yes, that was definitely the same kind of depression and it lasted that long.\n\nI’m not saying it never happened, because my memory of that stuff isn’t perfect. I don’t really mark it on a calendar or anything. But I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods. Some of them might have been more like stress, or being tired, or just having a bad run of days.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear past episode I can identify like that before this one.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time when a doctor or therapist told you that you were depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI’ve talked to people before—like, not always regularly, and sometimes it was more just because I was having a hard time or needed to sort things out—but I don’t remember anyone sitting me down and saying, “You are depressed,” or giving it to me like an official thing. Maybe they used words like stress or feeling low, or asked me about my mood, but I can’t point to a time before this where a doctor or therapist specifically told me I was depressed.\n\nI guess it’s possible I’m forgetting some wording from years ago, because I don’t always remember the exact language people use in appointments. But if you mean, was I diagnosed or clearly told that before this started, then no, I don’t really have that memory.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from the mortgage, did something stressful happen around the time these feelings began?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this sense that I’ve messed things up, especially for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep thinking that was my fault, like I pushed us into something I should’ve known better about, and now every bill or little problem with the house feels like proof that I’ve let them down.\n\nAnd it’s not just at home. At work, if I make even a small mistake—like getting an order wrong or forgetting something for a table—I just immediately go to this place in my head where it means I’m useless. Like it’s not just “I made a mistake,” it’s “of course you did, because you’re no good.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels true. I keep feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself somehow.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after taking on the mortgage?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s when it really started. Not like the exact day we signed the papers, but after we took on the condo mortgage it just kind of settled in and kept building. It’s been going on for about… a year and eight months now, I’d say.  \n\nAt first I thought it was just normal stress, you know, like anyone would worry about money and bills and whether they made the right decision. But it didn’t really ease up. It became this constant thing in the back of my mind, like every problem somehow pointed back to me making that choice.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were resolved tomorrow, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would just disappear, no. I mean… it would help, obviously. If the mortgage were somehow fixed tomorrow, I’d probably feel some relief, like one big thing wasn’t sitting on my chest all the time.\n\nBut the way I feel about it has kind of spread, if that makes sense. It’s not only “we have this mortgage and that’s stressful.” It’s more like I took that as proof that I let my family down, and then I started seeing everything else through that same lens. Like if something small happens at work, if I mess up an order or forget something for a table, I don’t just think, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I go straight to, “See, you’re useless. This is what you do. You ruin things.” And then I feel like whatever bad thing happens after that, I probably deserve it somehow.\n\nSo… resolving the mortgage would take away one big trigger, I guess. But I’m not sure it would fix the part of me that keeps turning everything into evidence that I’ve failed people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest a lot of the time. Like I’m moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAt work I can still carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say the little polite things you’re supposed to say, but it feels kind of… mechanical. Like I’m doing the part of a person who’s fine, but inside I don’t really feel much that’s good. It’s more like I’m just getting through each hour.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s kind of embarrassing to admit because it sounds small, but I notice it in those little everyday things.\n\nLike at work, I used to enjoy joking around with the regulars. There are people who come in all the time, and I used to remember their little stories or tease them about ordering the same thing, just harmless stuff. It made the shift feel a little lighter. Now I mostly just keep my face straight, take the order, bring the food, say “sure” or “no problem,” and that’s it. I’m not rude, I don’t think, but I don’t really feel that pull to connect or make it fun anymore.\n\nAnd after shifts, I used to text my brother, even if it was just something stupid that happened at work or a picture of something funny. Now I’ll see his message sitting there and I just… leave it. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel the energy or the interest to answer, even when part of me knows I should. Then too much time passes and it feels harder to respond, so I let it sit longer.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest in things I used to actually get something out of. I still do what I have to do, but I don’t really reach for things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t have a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.\n\nBut… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. Or at least it’s the one I’m really noticing now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. Or at least it’s the one I’m really noticing now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to “most of my adult life.” It feels more like something that comes up at certain times, not something I can clearly say has always been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m trying not to overcomplicate it, but I still don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nBefore this current thing, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of dragged out, where I wasn’t myself and I didn’t want to deal with much. But when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” and “like this,” that’s where I get stuck. I can’t really point to a specific stretch and say, yes, that was definitely the same kind of depression and it lasted that long.\n\nI’m not saying it never happened, because my memory of that stuff isn’t perfect. I don’t really mark it on a calendar or anything. But I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods. Some of them might have been more like stress, or being tired, or just having a bad run of days.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear past episode I can identify like that before this one.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time when a doctor or therapist told you that you were depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI’ve talked to people before—like, not always regularly, and sometimes it was more just because I was having a hard time or needed to sort things out—but I don’t remember anyone sitting me down and saying, “You are depressed,” or giving it to me like an official thing. Maybe they used words like stress or feeling low, or asked me about my mood, but I can’t point to a time before this where a doctor or therapist specifically told me I was depressed.\n\nI guess it’s possible I’m forgetting some wording from years ago, because I don’t always remember the exact language people use in appointments. Aside from the mortgage, I can’t think of one clear thing that happened right around then where I’d say, “That’s what started it.”\n\nI mean, life was still life—work stuff, bills, normal pressure, all of that—but nothing is jumping out at me as some big event or crisis besides the mortgage situation. Sometimes I worry I’m missing something because when you ask it that way, I start scanning back through everything, like maybe there was an argument or a bad week at work or something, but I don’t have a specific thing I can point to.\n\nSo… no, not that I can remember. No, I don’t think so. Not for that long.

Since it started—after I took on the condo mortgage—it’s been pretty continuous in one way or another. I mean, it hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, and I’ve had better days or maybe even a better week here and there, where I could get through things a little more normally. But if you mean really back to my usual self, like how I was before all of this, and staying that way for two months straight… no, I can’t say that happened.

It’s been about a year and eight months now, I think. I always tie it to the mortgage because that’s when it really seemed to begin. Since then, I don’t remember a solid stretch where I felt like, “Okay, I’m back to normal now,” and then stayed that way for a couple months.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this sense that I’ve messed things up, especially for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep thinking that was my fault, like I pushed us into something I should’ve known better about, and now every bill or little problem with the house feels like proof that I’ve let them down.\n\nAnd it’s not just at home. At work, if I make even a small mistake—like getting an order wrong or forgetting something for a table—I just immediately go to this place in my head where it means I’m useless. Like it’s not just “I made a mistake,” it’s “of course you did, because you’re no good.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels true. I keep feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself somehow.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after taking on the mortgage?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s when it really started. Not like the exact day we signed the papers, but after we took on the condo mortgage it just kind of settled in and kept building. It’s been going on for about… a year and eight months now, I’d say.  \n\nAt first I thought it was just normal stress, you know, like anyone would worry about money and bills and whether they made the right decision. But it didn’t really ease up. It became this constant thing in the back of my mind, like every problem somehow pointed back to me making that choice.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were resolved tomorrow, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would just disappear, no. I mean… it would help, obviously. If the mortgage were somehow fixed tomorrow, I’d probably feel some relief, like one big thing wasn’t sitting on my chest all the time.\n\nBut the way I feel about it has kind of spread, if that makes sense. It’s not only “we have this mortgage and that’s stressful.” It’s more like I took that as proof that I let my family down, and then I started seeing everything else through that same lens. Like if something small happens at work, if I mess up an order or forget something for a table, I don’t just think, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I go straight to, “See, you’re useless. This is what you do. You ruin things.” And then I feel like whatever bad thing happens after that, I probably deserve it somehow.\n\nSo… resolving the mortgage would take away one big trigger, I guess. But I’m not sure it would fix the part of me that keeps turning everything into evidence that I’ve failed people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest a lot of the time. Like I’m moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAt work I can still carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say the little polite things you’re supposed to say, but it feels kind of… mechanical. Like I’m doing the part of a person who’s fine, but inside I don’t really feel much that’s good. It’s more like I’m just getting through each hour.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s kind of embarrassing to admit because it sounds small, but I notice it in those little everyday things.\n\nLike at work, I used to enjoy joking around with the regulars. There are people who come in all the time, and I used to remember their little stories or tease them about ordering the same thing, just harmless stuff. It made the shift feel a little lighter. Now I mostly just keep my face straight, take the order, bring the food, say “sure” or “no problem,” and that’s it. I’m not rude, I don’t think, but I don’t really feel that pull to connect or make it fun anymore.\n\nAnd after shifts, I used to text my brother, even if it was just something stupid that happened at work or a picture of something funny. Now I’ll see his message sitting there and I just… leave it. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel the energy or the interest to answer, even when part of me knows I should. Then too much time passes and it feels harder to respond, so I let it sit longer.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest in things I used to actually get something out of. I still do what I have to do, but I don’t really reach for things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t have a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.\n\nBut… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. Or at least it’s the one I’m really noticing now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to “most of my adult life.” It feels more like something that comes up at certain times, not something I can clearly say has always been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m trying not to overcomplicate it, but I still don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nBefore this current thing, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of dragged out, where I wasn’t myself and I didn’t want to deal with much. But when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” and “like this,” that’s where I get stuck. I can’t really point to a specific stretch and say, yes, that was definitely the same kind of depression and it lasted that long.\n\nI’m not saying it never happened, because my memory of that stuff isn’t perfect. I don’t really mark it on a calendar or anything. But I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods. Some of them might have been more like stress, or being tired, or just having a bad run of days.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear past episode I can identify like that before this one.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time when a doctor or therapist told you that you were depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI’ve talked to people before—like, not always regularly, and sometimes it was more just because I was having a hard time or needed to sort things out—but I don’t remember anyone sitting me down and saying, “You are depressed,” or giving it to me like an official thing. Maybe they used words like stress or feeling low, or asked me about my mood, but I can’t point to a time before this where a doctor or therapist specifically told me I was depressed.\n\nI guess it’s possible I’m forgetting some wording from years ago, because I don’t always remember the exact language people use in appointments. But if you mean, was I diagnosed or clearly told that before this started, then no, I don’t really have that memory.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the mortgage, did something stressful happen around the time these feelings began?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Aside from the mortgage, I can’t think of one clear thing that happened right around then where I’d say, “That’s what started it.”\n\nI mean, life was still life—work stuff, bills, normal pressure, all of that—but nothing is jumping out at me as some big event or crisis besides the mortgage situation. Sometimes I worry I’m missing something because when you ask it that way, I start scanning back through everything, like maybe there was an argument or a bad week at work or something, but I don’t have a specific thing I can point to.\n\nSo… no, not that I can remember. The mortgage is the main stressful thing I connect with that time.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, has there been a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for that long.\n\nSince it started—after I took on the condo mortgage—it’s been pretty continuous in one way or another. I mean, it hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, and I’ve had better days or maybe even a better week here and there, where I could get through things a little more normally. But if you mean really back to my usual self, like how I was before all of this, and staying that way for two months straight… no, I can’t say that happened.\n\nIt’s been about a year and eight months now, I think. I always tie it to the mortgage because that’s when it really seemed to begin. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time when a doctor or therapist told you that you were depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI’ve talked to people before—like, not always regularly, and sometimes it was more just because I was having a hard time or needed to sort things out—but I don’t remember anyone sitting me down and saying, “You are depressed,” or giving it to me like an official thing. Maybe they used words like stress or feeling low, or asked me about my mood, but I can’t point to a time before this where a doctor or therapist specifically told me I was depressed.\n\nI guess it’s possible I’m forgetting some wording from years ago, because I don’t always remember the exact language people use in appointments. But if you mean, was I diagnosed or clearly told that before this started, then no, I don’t really have that memory.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the mortgage, did something stressful happen around the time these feelings began?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Aside from the mortgage, I can’t think of one clear thing that happened right around then where I’d say, “That’s what started it.”\n\nI mean, life was still life—work stuff, bills, normal pressure, all of that—but nothing is jumping out at me as some big event or crisis besides the mortgage situation. Sometimes I worry I’m missing something because when you ask it that way, I start scanning back through everything, like maybe there was an argument or a bad week at work or something, but I don’t have a specific thing I can point to.\n\nSo… no, not that I can remember. The mortgage is the main stressful thing I connect with that time.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, has there been a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for that long.\n\nSince it started—after I took on the condo mortgage—it’s been pretty continuous in one way or another. I mean, it hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, and I’ve had better days or maybe even a better week here and there, where I could get through things a little more normally. But if you mean really back to my usual self, like how I was before all of this, and staying that way for two months straight… no, I can’t say that happened.\n\nIt’s been about a year and eight months now, I think. I always tie it to the mortgage because that’s when it really seemed to begin. Since then, I don’t remember a solid stretch where I felt like, “Okay, I’m back to normal now,” and then stayed that way for a couple months.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you feel this guilty about the mortgage and other mistakes, have you ever believed something that others said wasn’t true—like that you had ruined your family beyond repair or deserved to be punished?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down by taking on this mortgage, like every small mistake at work proves I’m useless and deserve whatever happens to me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say it in that exact way, like “ruined beyond repair,” but I do get stuck on the idea that I’ve really let my family down by taking on this mortgage. It feels bigger than just a bad financial decision sometimes. It feels like I put everyone in a worse position because of me, and then I start thinking that says something about who I am.

And it’s not just the mortgage. At work, if I make some small mistake—like I forget something at a table, or I mess up an order, or someone looks annoyed—it feels like proof. Like, “See, you’re useless. You can’t even do this right.” I know other people might say it’s just a mistake and everyone does that, but in my head it turns into this whole thing where I deserve whatever happens to me because I caused it.

So… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that, that I deserve to be punished in some way, or that bad things happening to me would be fair because I made these mistakes. I don’t always say it out loud because it sounds extreme when I hear myself say it, but that’s how it feels when I’m in it.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this sense that I’ve messed things up, especially for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep thinking that was my fault, like I pushed us into something I should’ve known better about, and now every bill or little problem with the house feels like proof that I’ve let them down.\n\nAnd it’s not just at home. At work, if I make even a small mistake—like getting an order wrong or forgetting something for a table—I just immediately go to this place in my head where it means I’m useless. Like it’s not just “I made a mistake,” it’s “of course you did, because you’re no good.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels true. I keep feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself somehow.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after taking on the mortgage?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s when it really started. Not like the exact day we signed the papers, but after we took on the condo mortgage it just kind of settled in and kept building. It’s been going on for about… a year and eight months now, I’d say.  \n\nAt first I thought it was just normal stress, you know, like anyone would worry about money and bills and whether they made the right decision. But it didn’t really ease up. It became this constant thing in the back of my mind, like every problem somehow pointed back to me making that choice.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were resolved tomorrow, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would just disappear, no. I mean… it would help, obviously. If the mortgage were somehow fixed tomorrow, I’d probably feel some relief, like one big thing wasn’t sitting on my chest all the time.\n\nBut the way I feel about it has kind of spread, if that makes sense. It’s not only “we have this mortgage and that’s stressful.” It’s more like I took that as proof that I let my family down, and then I started seeing everything else through that same lens. Like if something small happens at work, if I mess up an order or forget something for a table, I don’t just think, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I go straight to, “See, you’re useless. This is what you do. You ruin things.” And then I feel like whatever bad thing happens after that, I probably deserve it somehow.\n\nSo… resolving the mortgage would take away one big trigger, I guess. But I’m not sure it would fix the part of me that keeps turning everything into evidence that I’ve failed people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest a lot of the time. Like I’m moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAt work I can still carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say the little polite things you’re supposed to say, but it feels kind of… mechanical. Like I’m doing the part of a person who’s fine, but inside I don’t really feel much that’s good. It’s more like I’m just getting through each hour.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s kind of embarrassing to admit because it sounds small, but I notice it in those little everyday things.\n\nLike at work, I used to enjoy joking around with the regulars. There are people who come in all the time, and I used to remember their little stories or tease them about ordering the same thing, just harmless stuff. It made the shift feel a little lighter. Now I mostly just keep my face straight, take the order, bring the food, say “sure” or “no problem,” and that’s it. I’m not rude, I don’t think, but I don’t really feel that pull to connect or make it fun anymore.\n\nAnd after shifts, I used to text my brother, even if it was just something stupid that happened at work or a picture of something funny. Now I’ll see his message sitting there and I just… leave it. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel the energy or the interest to answer, even when part of me knows I should. Then too much time passes and it feels harder to respond, so I let it sit longer.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest in things I used to actually get something out of. I still do what I have to do, but I don’t really reach for things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t have a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.\n\nBut… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. Or at least it’s the one I’m really noticing now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to “most of my adult life.” It feels more like something that comes up at certain times, not something I can clearly say has always been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m trying not to overcomplicate it, but I still don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nBefore this current thing, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of dragged out, where I wasn’t myself and I didn’t want to deal with much. But when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” and “like this,” that’s where I get stuck. I can’t really point to a specific stretch and say, yes, that was definitely the same kind of depression and it lasted that long.\n\nI’m not saying it never happened, because my memory of that stuff isn’t perfect. I don’t really mark it on a calendar or anything. But I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods. Some of them might have been more like stress, or being tired, or just having a bad run of days.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear past episode I can identify like that before this one.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time when a doctor or therapist told you that you were depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI’ve talked to people before—like, not always regularly, and sometimes it was more just because I was having a hard time or needed to sort things out—but I don’t remember anyone sitting me down and saying, “You are depressed,” or giving it to me like an official thing. Maybe they used words like stress or feeling low, or asked me about my mood, but I can’t point to a time before this where a doctor or therapist specifically told me I was depressed.\n\nI guess it’s possible I’m forgetting some wording from years ago, because I don’t always remember the exact language people use in appointments. But if you mean, was I diagnosed or clearly told that before this started, then no, I don’t really have that memory.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the mortgage, did something stressful happen around the time these feelings began?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Aside from the mortgage, I can’t think of one clear thing that happened right around then where I’d say, “That’s what started it.”\n\nI mean, life was still life—work stuff, bills, normal pressure, all of that—but nothing is jumping out at me as some big event or crisis besides the mortgage situation. Sometimes I worry I’m missing something because when you ask it that way, I start scanning back through everything, like maybe there was an argument or a bad week at work or something, but I don’t have a specific thing I can point to.\n\nSo… no, not that I can remember. The mortgage is the main stressful thing I connect with that time.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, has there been a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for that long.\n\nSince it started—after I took on the condo mortgage—it’s been pretty continuous in one way or another.
