It became this constant thing in the back of my mind, like every problem somehow pointed back to me making that choice.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were resolved tomorrow, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would just disappear, no. I mean… it would help, obviously. If the mortgage were somehow fixed tomorrow, I’d probably feel some relief, like one big thing wasn’t sitting on my chest all the time.\n\nBut the way I feel about it has kind of spread, if that makes sense. It’s not only “we have this mortgage and that’s stressful.” It’s more like I took that as proof that I let my family down, and then I started seeing everything else through that same lens. Like if something small happens at work, if I mess up an order or forget something for a table, I don’t just think, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I go straight to, “See, you’re useless. This is what you do. You ruin things.” And then I feel like whatever bad thing happens after that, I probably deserve it somehow.\n\nSo… resolving the mortgage would take away one big trigger, I guess. But I’m not sure it would fix the part of me that keeps turning everything into evidence that I’ve failed people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest a lot of the time. Like I’m moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAt work I can still carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say the little polite things you’re supposed to say, but it feels kind of… mechanical. Like I’m doing the part of a person who’s fine, but inside I don’t really feel much that’s good. It’s more like I’m just getting through each hour.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s kind of embarrassing to admit because it sounds small, but I notice it in those little everyday things.\n\nLike at work, I used to enjoy joking around with the regulars. There are people who come in all the time, and I used to remember their little stories or tease them about ordering the same thing, just harmless stuff. It made the shift feel a little lighter. Now I mostly just keep my face straight, take the order, bring the food, say “sure” or “no problem,” and that’s it. I’m not rude, I don’t think, but I don’t really feel that pull to connect or make it fun anymore.\n\nAnd after shifts, I used to text my brother, even if it was just something stupid that happened at work or a picture of something funny. Now I’ll see his message sitting there and I just… leave it. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel the energy or the interest to answer, even when part of me knows I should. Then too much time passes and it feels harder to respond, so I let it sit longer.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest in things I used to actually get something out of. I still do what I have to do, but I don’t really reach for things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t have a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.\n\nBut… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. Or at least it’s the one I’m really noticing now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to “most of my adult life.” It feels more like something that comes up at certain times, not something I can clearly say has always been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m trying not to overcomplicate it, but I still don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nBefore this current thing, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of dragged out, where I wasn’t myself and I didn’t want to deal with much. But when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” and “like this,” that’s where I get stuck. I can’t really point to a specific stretch and say, yes, that was definitely the same kind of depression and it lasted that long.\n\nI’m not saying it never happened, because my memory of that stuff isn’t perfect. I don’t really mark it on a calendar or anything. But I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods. Some of them might have been more like stress, or being tired, or just having a bad run of days.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear past episode I can identify like that before this one.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time when a doctor or therapist told you that you were depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI’ve talked to people before—like, not always regularly, and sometimes it was more just because I was having a hard time or needed to sort things out—but I don’t remember anyone sitting me down and saying, “You are depressed,” or giving it to me like an official thing. Maybe they used words like stress or feeling low, or asked me about my mood, but I can’t point to a time before this where a doctor or therapist specifically told me I was depressed.\n\nI guess it’s possible I’m forgetting some wording from years ago, because I don’t always remember the exact language people use in appointments. But if you mean, was I diagnosed or clearly told that before this started, then no, I don’t really have that memory.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the mortgage, did something stressful happen around the time these feelings began?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Aside from the mortgage, I can’t think of one clear thing that happened right around then where I’d say, “That’s what started it.”\n\nI mean, life was still life—work stuff, bills, normal pressure, all of that—but nothing is jumping out at me as some big event or crisis besides the mortgage situation. Sometimes I worry I’m missing something because when you ask it that way, I start scanning back through everything, like maybe there was an argument or a bad week at work or something, but I don’t have a specific thing I can point to.\n\nSo… no, not that I can remember. The mortgage is the main stressful thing I connect with that time.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, has there been a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for that long.\n\nSince it started—after I took on the condo mortgage—it’s been pretty continuous in one way or another. I mean, it hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, and I’ve had better days or maybe even a better week here and there, where I could get through things a little more normally. But if you mean really back to my usual self, like how I was before all of this, and staying that way for two months straight… no, I can’t say that happened.\n\nIt’s been about a year and eight months now, I think. I always tie it to the mortgage because that’s when it really seemed to begin. Since then, I don’t remember a solid stretch where I felt like, “Okay, I’m back to normal now,” and then stayed that way for a couple months.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this guilty about the mortgage and other mistakes, have you ever believed something that others said wasn’t true—like that you had ruined your family beyond repair or deserved to be punished?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say it in that exact way, like “ruined beyond repair,” but I do get stuck on the idea that I’ve really let my family down by taking on this mortgage. It feels bigger than just a bad financial decision sometimes. It feels like I put everyone in a worse position because of me, and then I start thinking that says something about who I am.\n\nAnd it’s not just the mortgage. At work, if I make some small mistake—like I forget something at a table, or I mess up an order, or someone looks annoyed—it feels like proof. Like, “See, you’re useless. You can’t even do this right.” I know other people might say it’s just a mistake and everyone does that, but in my head it turns into this whole thing where I deserve whatever happens to me because I caused it.\n\nSo… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that, that I deserve to be punished in some way, or that bad things happening to me would be fair because I made these mistakes. I don’t always say it out loud because it sounds extreme when I hear myself say it, but that’s how it feels when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Are you still dealing with the mortgage as an ongoing stress in your life right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, it’s still there in the sense that I still think about it, and when bills or anything financial comes up, my mind goes right back to it. But I don’t know if I’d say there’s some new mortgage crisis happening every day, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like it’s become this ongoing thing in the background that I keep circling back to.\n\nLike, even on a normal day, if nothing specific happens, I can still find myself thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What if this keeps affecting everyone?” So yes, in that way, I’m still dealing with it. I don’t want to pretend I wouldn’t. But I don’t think that would suddenly make me feel like myself again. Most days there’s still this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like I’m just going through the motions. At work I’m carrying plates, smiling at people because I’m supposed to smile, saying the right things, trying to seem normal… but it doesn’t really feel connected to anything good inside me.\n\nSo I guess what I’m saying is, the mortgage makes it worse, or gives it something to focus on. But even if that problem disappeared, I think that heavy, blank feeling would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, did you ever have a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m trying to think back before the mortgage became the main thing I blamed everything on, and… yes, there were stretches where I felt depressed most days.\n\nIt wasn’t always tied to one specific problem. It was more like waking up already feeling heavy, like there was this empty weight sitting in my chest before the day even started. And I’d go to work and do what I had to do—carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say “How’s everything tasting?” and all that—but it felt like I was acting out the motions more than actually being there.\n\nI don’t know if I was counting the days at the time, like, “This has been exactly two weeks,” but it definitely wasn’t just a bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It would go on long enough that it started to feel normal, almost like that blank, heavy feeling was just how I was going to feel. Even if nothing was actively happening that day, I’d still feel low, like I couldn’t really reach anything good inside myself.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, did there ever come a time when that depression lifted and you felt back to your usual self for at least two months before becoming depressed again?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I’m not sure I can say it ever completely went away, if I’m being honest. There were times before the mortgage where it wasn’t as loud, maybe, or I could get through the day a little easier, but that heavy, empty feeling… it was still there a lot of the time.

Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. It feels like I put everyone in a worse position because of me, and then I start thinking that says something about who I am.\n\nAnd it’s not just the mortgage. I don’t always say it out loud because it sounds extreme when I hear myself say it, but that’s how it feels when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Are you still dealing with the mortgage as an ongoing stress in your life right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, it’s still there in the sense that I still think about it, and when bills or anything financial comes up, my mind goes right back to it. At work I’m carrying plates, smiling at people because I’m supposed to smile, saying the right things, trying to seem normal… but it doesn’t really feel connected to anything good inside me.\n\nSo I guess what I’m saying is, the mortgage makes it worse, or gives it something to focus on. But even if that problem disappeared, I think that heavy, blank feeling would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, did you ever have a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m trying to think back before the mortgage became the main thing I blamed everything on, and… yes, there were stretches where I felt depressed most days.\n\nIt wasn’t always tied to one specific problem. It was more like waking up already feeling heavy, like there was this empty weight sitting in my chest before the day even started. And I’d go to work and do what I had to do—carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say “How’s everything tasting?” and all that—but it felt like I was acting out the motions more than actually being there.\n\nI don’t know if I was counting the days at the time, like, “This has been exactly two weeks,” but it definitely wasn’t just a bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It would go on long enough that it started to feel normal, almost like that blank, heavy feeling was just how I was going to feel. Even if nothing was actively happening that day, I’d still feel low, like I couldn’t really reach anything good inside myself.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, did there ever come a time when that depression lifted and you felt back to your usual self for at least two months before becoming depressed again?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I’m trying to look back and separate things out, but it all kind of blends together in my memory.\n\nThere were definitely times where I functioned better, or I seemed more normal from the outside. Like I’d go to work, keep up with things, maybe joke around a little more, not feel quite so weighed down every single day. But whether that was me fully back to my usual self for two whole months… I don’t know. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing.\n\nI think there were breaks where it wasn’t as intense, maybe where I could breathe a little easier. But I can’t clearly point to a stretch and say, “Yes, for at least two months I was completely back to myself before it came back.” It’s more blurry than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, was there ever a time when a depression like this lasted at least two weeks and then completely went away before coming back again?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it ever completely went away, if I’m being honest. There were times before the mortgage where it wasn’t as loud, maybe, or I could get through the day a little easier, but that heavy, empty feeling… it was still there a lot of the time.\n\nMost days it felt like I’d wake up with this weight in my chest, and then I’d just go through the motions. Like at the restaurant, I’d be carrying plates, smiling at tables, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, but it didn’t feel like it was coming from anywhere real. It was more like I was performing being okay.\n\nSo, if you mean did I have depression for a couple weeks and then feel totally normal again before it came back, I don’t know that I can clearly say that. It was more like it would ease up and then get heavier again, but I don’t remember a clean break where I felt completely like myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even if nothing was actively happening that day, I’d still feel low, like I couldn’t really reach anything good inside myself.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, did there ever come a time when that depression lifted and you felt back to your usual self for at least two months before becoming depressed again?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I’m trying to look back and separate things out, but it all kind of blends together in my memory.\n\nThere were definitely times where I functioned better, or I seemed more normal from the outside. Like I’d go to work, keep up with things, maybe joke around a little more, not feel quite so weighed down every single day. But whether that was me fully back to my usual self for two whole months… I don’t know. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing.\n\nI think there were breaks where it wasn’t as intense, maybe where I could breathe a little easier. But I can’t clearly point to a stretch and say, “Yes, for at least two months I was completely back to myself before it came back.” It’s more blurry than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, was there ever a time when a depression like this lasted at least two weeks and then completely went away before coming back again?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it ever completely went away, if I’m being honest. There were times before the mortgage where it wasn’t as loud, maybe, or I could get through the day a little easier, but that heavy, empty feeling… it was still there a lot of the time.\n\nMost days it felt like I’d wake up with this weight in my chest, and then I’d just go through the motions. Like at the restaurant, I’d be carrying plates, smiling at tables, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, but it didn’t feel like it was coming from anywhere real. It was more like I was performing being okay.\n\nSo, if you mean did I have depression for a couple weeks and then feel totally normal again before it came back, I don’t know that I can clearly say that. It was more like it would ease up and then get heavier again, but I don’t remember a clean break where I felt completely like myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt depressed more days than not for at least two years?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this sense that I’ve messed things up, especially for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep thinking that was my fault, like I pushed us into something I should’ve known better about, and now every bill or little problem with the house feels like proof that I’ve let them down.\n\nAnd it’s not just at home. At work, if I make even a small mistake—like getting an order wrong or forgetting something for a table—I just immediately go to this place in my head where it means I’m useless. Like it’s not just “I made a mistake,” it’s “of course you did, because you’re no good.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels true. I keep feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself somehow.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after taking on the mortgage?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s when it really started. Not like the exact day we signed the papers, but after we took on the condo mortgage it just kind of settled in and kept building. It’s been going on for about… a year and eight months now, I’d say.  \n\nAt first I thought it was just normal stress, you know, like anyone would worry about money and bills and whether they made the right decision. But it didn’t really ease up. It became this constant thing in the back of my mind, like every problem somehow pointed back to me making that choice.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were resolved tomorrow, do you think these feelings would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would just disappear, no. I mean… it would help, obviously. If the mortgage were somehow fixed tomorrow, I’d probably feel some relief, like one big thing wasn’t sitting on my chest all the time.\n\nBut the way I feel about it has kind of spread, if that makes sense. It’s not only “we have this mortgage and that’s stressful.” It’s more like I took that as proof that I let my family down, and then I started seeing everything else through that same lens. Like if something small happens at work, if I mess up an order or forget something for a table, I don’t just think, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I go straight to, “See, you’re useless. This is what you do. You ruin things.” And then I feel like whatever bad thing happens after that, I probably deserve it somehow.\n\nSo… resolving the mortgage would take away one big trigger, I guess. But I’m not sure it would fix the part of me that keeps turning everything into evidence that I’ve failed people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest a lot of the time. Like I’m moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAt work I can still carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say the little polite things you’re supposed to say, but it feels kind of… mechanical. Like I’m doing the part of a person who’s fine, but inside I don’t really feel much that’s good. It’s more like I’m just getting through each hour.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Now I mostly just keep my face straight, take the order, bring the food, say “sure” or “no problem,” and that’s it. I’m not rude, I don’t think, but I don’t really feel that pull to connect or make it fun anymore.\n\nAnd after shifts, I used to text my brother, even if it was just something stupid that happened at work or a picture of something funny. Now I’ll see his message sitting there and I just… leave it. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel the energy or the interest to answer, even when part of me knows I should. Then too much time passes and it feels harder to respond, so I let it sit longer.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest in things I used to actually get something out of. I still do what I have to do, but I don’t really reach for things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t have a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and figured out a way to hurt myself. It’s not like I’m making preparations.\n\nBut… I do have those thoughts sometimes, and I hate saying that out loud. Usually it’s at night, or really early in the morning when I know I have to be up for an opening shift and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. Everything feels so quiet, and my mind just starts going over all the ways I’ve messed things up, and I’ll think… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like not exactly that I want to do something to myself, but more like I wish I could just not have to keep doing this, not have to keep failing at everything.\n\nIt scares me a little because I know that’s not a normal place for my mind to go. But no, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s more like wanting to disappear or not wake up for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times before where I felt low or worn down, like anybody does, I guess, and there were stretches where I wasn’t doing great. But whether it was like this, and whether it was every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that clearly.\n\nI don’t keep track of it very well. Looking back, everything kind of blurs together, and I don’t want to say yes if it was just a bad week or stress or me being tired. This feels… more constant, I think. Or at least it’s the one I’m really noticing now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a specific past period and say, “Yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “a lot” or about many different things for no reason.\n\nMost of the time if I’m worried, I can usually point to something, like work or money or whether I’m falling behind on something I was supposed to handle. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of every possible disaster all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes my mind does kind of keep chewing on things longer than it needs to, but I haven’t really thought of myself as a constant worrier. It’s more like… I get stuck on whatever is right in front of me, if that makes sense. So I don’t know. Maybe a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s this big, across-the-board worry about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally felt this way about yourself—like you’re inadequate or likely to be judged negatively by others—for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most of my adult life.” That feels like a big statement, and I’m not sure I can honestly look back and say it’s always been that way.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I felt awkward or like people were judging me, especially at work or in situations where I feel like I’m being watched or evaluated. And I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But I don’t know that it’s been this constant thing since I was young, like a lifelong pattern where I always felt inadequate around everyone.\n\nIt kind of depends on what’s going on. If I’m already feeling low or I mess something up, then yeah, I can start assuming people think badly of me. But there have also been plenty of times where I didn’t really think about it that much, or I could just get through things without feeling like everyone was judging me.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to “most of my adult life.” It feels more like something that comes up at certain times, not something I can clearly say has always been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m trying not to overcomplicate it, but I still don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nBefore this current thing, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of dragged out, where I wasn’t myself and I didn’t want to deal with much. But when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” and “like this,” that’s where I get stuck. I can’t really point to a specific stretch and say, yes, that was definitely the same kind of depression and it lasted that long.\n\nI’m not saying it never happened, because my memory of that stuff isn’t perfect. I don’t really mark it on a calendar or anything. But I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods. Some of them might have been more like stress, or being tired, or just having a bad run of days.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear past episode I can identify like that before this one.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time when a doctor or therapist told you that you were depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI’ve talked to people before—like, not always regularly, and sometimes it was more just because I was having a hard time or needed to sort things out—but I don’t remember anyone sitting me down and saying, “You are depressed,” or giving it to me like an official thing. Maybe they used words like stress or feeling low, or asked me about my mood, but I can’t point to a time before this where a doctor or therapist specifically told me I was depressed.\n\nI guess it’s possible I’m forgetting some wording from years ago, because I don’t always remember the exact language people use in appointments. But if you mean, was I diagnosed or clearly told that before this started, then no, I don’t really have that memory.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the mortgage, did something stressful happen around the time these feelings began?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Aside from the mortgage, I can’t think of one clear thing that happened right around then where I’d say, “That’s what started it.”\n\nI mean, life was still life—work stuff, bills, normal pressure, all of that—but nothing is jumping out at me as some big event or crisis besides the mortgage situation. Sometimes I worry I’m missing something because when you ask it that way, I start scanning back through everything, like maybe there was an argument or a bad week at work or something, but I don’t have a specific thing I can point to.\n\nSo… no, not that I can remember. The mortgage is the main stressful thing I connect with that time.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, has there been a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for that long.\n\nSince it started—after I took on the condo mortgage—it’s been pretty continuous in one way or another. I mean, it hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, and I’ve had better days or maybe even a better week here and there, where I could get through things a little more normally. I always tie it to the mortgage because that’s when it really seemed to begin. Since then, I don’t remember a solid stretch where I felt like, “Okay, I’m back to normal now,” and then stayed that way for a couple months.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this guilty about the mortgage and other mistakes, have you ever believed something that others said wasn’t true—like that you had ruined your family beyond repair or deserved to be punished?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say it in that exact way, like “ruined beyond repair,” but I do get stuck on the idea that I’ve really let my family down by taking on this mortgage. It feels bigger than just a bad financial decision sometimes. It feels like I put everyone in a worse position because of me, and then I start thinking that says something about who I am.\n\nAnd it’s not just the mortgage. At work, if I make some small mistake—like I forget something at a table, or I mess up an order, or someone looks annoyed—it feels like proof. Like, “See, you’re useless. You can’t even do this right.” I know other people might say it’s just a mistake and everyone does that, but in my head it turns into this whole thing where I deserve whatever happens to me because I caused it.\n\nSo… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that, that I deserve to be punished in some way, or that bad things happening to me would be fair because I made these mistakes. I don’t always say it out loud because it sounds extreme when I hear myself say it, but that’s how it feels when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Are you still dealing with the mortgage as an ongoing stress in your life right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, it’s still there in the sense that I still think about it, and when bills or anything financial comes up, my mind goes right back to it. But I don’t know if I’d say there’s some new mortgage crisis happening every day, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like it’s become this ongoing thing in the background that I keep circling back to.\n\nLike, even on a normal day, if nothing specific happens, I can still find myself thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What if this keeps affecting everyone?” So yes, in that way, I’m still dealing with it. But I might be mixing up the actual stress of the mortgage with how much I keep blaming myself for it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage were no longer a problem, would you still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I don’t think the mortgage is the whole thing anymore, even if it’s the thing my mind keeps grabbing onto.\n\nLike, if someone magically fixed it tomorrow and said, “You don’t have to worry about that anymore,” I’d probably feel relieved, sure. I don’t want to pretend I wouldn’t. But I don’t think that would suddenly make me feel like myself again. Most days there’s still this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like I’m just going through the motions. At work I’m carrying plates, smiling at people because I’m supposed to smile, saying the right things, trying to seem normal… but it doesn’t really feel connected to anything good inside me.\n\nSo I guess what I’m saying is, the mortgage makes it worse, or gives it something to focus on. But even if that problem disappeared, I think that heavy, blank feeling would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, did you ever have a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m trying to think back before the mortgage became the main thing I blamed everything on, and… yes, there were stretches where I felt depressed most days.\n\nIt wasn’t always tied to one specific problem. It was more like waking up already feeling heavy, like there was this empty weight sitting in my chest before the day even started. And I’d go to work and do what I had to do—carry plates, take orders, smile at people, say “How’s everything tasting?” and all that—but it felt like I was acting out the motions more than actually being there.\n\nI don’t know if I was counting the days at the time, like, “This has been exactly two weeks,” but it definitely wasn’t just a bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It would go on long enough that it started to feel normal, almost like that blank, heavy feeling was just how I was going to feel. Even if nothing was actively happening that day, I’d still feel low, like I couldn’t really reach anything good inside myself.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, did there ever come a time when that depression lifted and you felt back to your usual self for at least two months before becoming depressed again?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I’m trying to look back and separate things out, but it all kind of blends together in my memory.\n\nThere were definitely times where I functioned better, or I seemed more normal from the outside. Like I’d go to work, keep up with things, maybe joke around a little more, not feel quite so weighed down every single day. But whether that was me fully back to my usual self for two whole months… I don’t know. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing.\n\nI think there were breaks where it wasn’t as intense, maybe where I could breathe a little easier. But I can’t clearly point to a stretch and say, “Yes, for at least two months I was completely back to myself before it came back.” It’s more blurry than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage, was there ever a time when a depression like this lasted at least two weeks and then completely went away before coming back again?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it ever completely went away, if I’m being honest. There were times before the mortgage where it wasn’t as loud, maybe, or I could get through the day a little easier, but that heavy, empty feeling… it was still there a lot of the time.\n\nMost days it felt like I’d wake up with this weight in my chest, and then I’d just go through the motions. Like at the restaurant, I’d be carrying plates, smiling at tables, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, but it didn’t feel like it was coming from anywhere real. It was more like I was performing being okay.\n\nSo, if you mean did I have depression for a couple weeks and then feel totally normal again before it came back, I don’t know that I can clearly say that. It was more like it would ease up and then get heavier again, but I don’t remember a clean break where I felt completely like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt depressed more days than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been a full two years, no. It’s been… close, maybe close enough that it feels like forever when I’m living inside it, but if I’m trying to be accurate, it’s more like about a year and eight months.\n\nIt really started after I took on the condo mortgage. That’s when the depressed feeling became more consistent—more days than not, like you said. Before that I had low stretches, but since the mortgage, it’s been this ongoing thing that’s just kind of settled over everything. So, not quite two years, but not just a short period either."
